Short Report From Central Office Nicola McIntyre 15 ￼15 Image by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C. Photo: K.L.
Did you know that your Constituent Society can apply to become an Accredited Training Society within ESH? Your Constituent Society would need to provide training that would consist of Basic Theoretical Hypnosis Training of at least 50 hours and Supervision in Hypnosis applications of at least 50 hours. Submission would require an application form to be completed along with content of training program, trainee criteria, trainee criteria & trainer CV’s and supervisor criteria and supervisor CV’s. This would then all get sent for evaluation and approval by the Committee on Educational Programmes in Europe (CEPE). Once approved a certificate would be issued valid for three years. If you would like anymore information please contact – mail@esh-hypnosis.eu ￼16
Ideas From the World of Clinical Hypnosis 17 ￼17 Image by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C.
CLINICAL CASES 18 B.A. (Psychology) - from D.I.T. in Detroit Michigan (on the dean's list).
M.A. - from the Hebrew University in Jerusalem (General Psychology, including various courses in educational Psych, clinical, and organizational psychology). About five years of specialization in psychological services:  Educational-Psychologist. Many years of experience in the school system, with school principals, teachers, students, and parents. (big part of that: As founder and manager of the psychological service). Many years of training and experience in stress and anxiety. At the same time, all these years in private practice (with children, teenagers, and adults). About the work I brought here: since most of my work was on the field only, I'm bringing here, two cases among the children and the youth that present difficulties, and the treatment is a little more complicated. The first one is with O.C.D and the second is with school phobia. Case Study 1 Presentation: A 10-Year-Old with Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder Case Overview: This case study examines a 10-year-old girl, referred to as Dana, who is experiencing Obsessive- Compulsive Disorder OCD. Dana is the daughter of a 42-year-old mother, who immigrated to Israel, from Russia, and works as a secretary at a college, and a 45-year-old father, who immigrated from Ukraine and works as a computer programmer. She also has a 7-year-old brother. Dana, a beautiful and charming fifth grader, accompanied her mother to the sessions.
Presenting Issues:
Dana's primary complaints included severe anxiety and numerous repetitive rituals. Examples of these rituals included spitting into a cup instead of swallowing, rubbing her foot on the floor, jumping, entering rooms with her right foot, and more. her anxiety, also manifested itself in great fear, when walking down the street and kept looking behind to see that there were no scary people there. ￼18 By Rachel Givati
Assessment and Methodology: Upon meeting Dana rapport was quickly established, and she was easy to communicate with. I employed a comprehensive Emotional-Behavioural-Social assessment, utilizing tools I developed over many years, alongside a detailed anamnesis. The assessment involved various tests, including:
Extended human figure   drawing
Sentence completion tasks
Recollection of three happy memories and three sad memories
A fairy tale exercise involving three gifts or wishes (tailored to the child's age)
Direct or indirect conversation, depending on the child's developmental level Therapeutic Approach: Based on the findings, I implemented a Cognitive Behavioural Therapy (CBT) approach with changing the status setting from negative to positive, and lots of positive reinforcement. The therapy aimed to enhance Dana's self-confidence and self-image. Hypnotic techniques were also integrated to manage anxiety and stress, including one hypnosis session towards the end of the process where Dana chose the "magic water" metaphor (out of options) "to wash away the "bad thoughts " while using "ego strengthening" technique,
Parental Involvement: Guidance was provided to Dana's parents to support her progress at home. Their cooperation was crucial in focusing on the treatment and achieving positive outcomes. Notably, A consistently looked forward to and enjoyed her sessions, with her mother observing improvements and reductions in ritualistic behaviours. Outcome and Follow-Up: The treatment lasted approximately seven weeks, with weekly sessions. Progress was gradual, leading to a complete cessation of rituals and a significant anxiety reduction. It was discovered that Dana’s OCD symptoms were triggered by a distressing experience during a family trip to Madrid, where she witnessed a homeless drunk individual. This traumatic event was further exacerbated by a similar experience in Tel Aviv, leading to heightened fear and compulsive behaviour with many repetitions of rituals, (as mentioned above). I would like to emphasize that, the fact that I had the cooperation of the child and her parents, contributed to a real success in treatment as described here, With a sense of accomplishment, to me, and them! Three months after the conclusion of treatment, Dana's mother reported that Dana was feeling very well, with no residual rituals or anxiety. This successful outcome underscores the importance of effective therapeutic strategies and strong parental involvement in managing and overcoming OCD. Case Study 2 Presentation: Adolescent School Refusal and Family Dynamics Case Overview: A 14-year-old girl was referred to me by her father due to six months of school refusal.
Background and Assessment: ￼19
The girl is the youngest of two children (brother-17 years old), for an older pair of parents. the mother was diagnosed (according to the father) as" low " - on the general intellectual functioning level. Though well-meaning and caring, the parents struggled with effective communication with their daughter. This lack of communication led to significant anger and frustration from the girl. Intervention: I began by providing a supportive space for the girl to express her frustrations and anger. The initial focus was on listening to her experiences and validating her feelings. Concurrently, efforts were made to bridge the communication gap between her and her parents. This involved helping the parents understand the generational differences and the importance of expressing their love more effectively. Therapeutic Approach: In addition to the "CBT" approach, there was also, one session of Hypnosis, where the work there, was, Ego strengthening and experiencing the new effective tools, at home with the parents, and in the school in the academic and social areas. Once the girl recognized that her parents cared for her, despite their communication challenges, we worked on enhancing her self-confidence. As her self-esteem improved, she gradually took on more responsibilities at home. Simultaneously, the parents were equipped with strategies to better understand their daughter's needs and to reinforce their parental authority. Outcome: After approximately three months of therapy, the girl enrolled in a private high school began attending regularly, and achieved commendable academic performance. The treatment concluded successfully, and the parents later reported that she graduated from high school with success. The process took about 4 months of a lot of effort… it finally brought good results.
After about 2 months of continuous support and reinforcement, she showed good and stable results, which were demonstrated by positive performance and consistently good grades. The all process took 6 months, and we finished with good feelings.
Follow-Up: Approximately ten years later, I encountered the girl's father, who shared that she had married, settled in the northern region, and was happily living her life. This case highlights the significance of addressing family communication issues and the impact of therapeutic interventions on improving both individual and family dynamics. ￼20
ADAPTING HYPNOTHERAPY FOR ELDERLY INDIVIDUALS WITH NEUROCOGNITIVE DISORDERS 21 Trained in hypnosis by Michel Kerouac at the Milton Erickson Institute of Quebec, I have since adapted this technique to support elderly patients with dementia in hospitals and care facilities. This approach has proven particularly effective in alleviating anxiety and creating a more serene environment for care. By implementing hypnotic tools, we can significantly reduce distress in patients with dementia, enabling caregivers to provide treatment under much calmer conditions. This method requires careful adjustment to the specific needs of each individual, as patients may exhibit various behavioural disorders. The positive impact extends beyond the patients themselves, notably improving the experience for both caregivers and family members. To share these insights and offer practical solutions, I co authored a book with Serge Sirvain, published by Dunod, which provides guidance on applying this method in various care settings.
Hypnosis and the elderly Various studies have demonstrated the effectiveness of the implementation of conversational hypnosis among seniors (Nightingale and Duff 2007. Wawrziczny et al., 2021, Bertoni 2020). This approach can be taught through short training courses that focus on: The “pacing” technique, which connects to the person by meeting them where they are. This involves peaceful breathing, a reassuring posture, and a hypnogenic monotone voice. The “sprinkling” of soothing words to create a sense of security, (e.g., “peacefully”, “quietly”, “calmly”, “gently” …). The creation of metaphors that can be integrated throughout the care process. Training is most effective when conducted within a care unit, allowing all team members to collectively integrate these new skills (Perennou, 2017, 2024. Floccia, 2020, 2024). It is crucial to emphasize that this tool should only be used within each healthcare professional's area of expertise. Hypnotic Tool: setting up a “Secure-Photography” intervention Due to neurodegenerative pathologies, many people can no longer name their places of emotional safety. It is necessary to identify those spaces upstream during informal conversations with the person or their relatives. This process should begin upon the resident's admission to the nursing home but can also be initiated by home care workers or family members. In a nursing home setting, this tool encourages staff to delve deeper into residents' life histories, uncovering previously unknown information about their past, profession, and interests. This research occurs naturally during initial encounters when the person arrives in the unit. During the initial set-up of the tool, the professionals of the unit distribute this work among themselves and posters of relevant information can be produced in a short time. Observation of the bedroom provides relevant information: Are there photos displayed other than those of grandchildren whom the elderly person may no longer recognize? Family photos should be be used cautiously, as nurses may not know the identity of each person or their relationship to the resident. ￼21 By Genevieve Perennou
The photos will preferably be chosen when the patient is aged between ten and thirty because memory is generally more vivid and accessible in that period of life. The appropriateness of the chosen photos is systematically and spontaneously verified. Nurses, pleased with their completed posters, often share them in the staff room, where colleagues can readily recognize the resident depicted. Implementing “Secure-Photography” The gathered information is then presented on an A4 sheet containing up to 4 photos. These often include images of the resident’s home, a significant journey, their occupation, animals, or a garden.... If the photo shows a specific place, it may be relevant to label it clearly so that all staff can correctly identify it: «the family home from 1950-1970, the farm, the village shop, the church of (name of) a village …”. These sheets are professionally presented and placed in the same location in all bedrooms within the nursing home. This allows each nurse to access the necessary information immediately upon entering the room. The care team thus has clear indications for directing the conversation, enabling them to adapt their communication approach with each elderly resident. During care procedures, the nurse assists the resident in dissociating from the immediate situation, guiding him to settle into a metaphorical "protective bubble of comfort". This technique helps to reduce anxiety and discomfort associated with care activities. Use of the Secure-Photography During care procedures, the induction of a trance state typically occurs within seconds through conversational hypnosis, as the elderly person with neuro-cognitive disorders is very often already in a dissociated state. S. Sirvain (2021) indicates that “many elderly patients with neurodegenerative pathologies are dissociated, in spontaneous or post- traumatic daily trance… Many patients, although physically present, appear to be elsewhere in their memories, absent from our reality and present. The eyes are turned inward. It looks like a presence/ absence, a dissociation.”   ￼22 ￼
By looking at these photographs, care staff can quickly identify significant places or themes in the resident's life, such as Mont Saint-Michel, the seaside, or their former home. This information provides healthcare professionals with several options for creating personalized metaphors to use during care procedures, enhancing the resident's comfort and engagement.
The caregiver selects one of the locations from the "Secure-Photography" sheet with which they feel comfortable creating a metaphor (Perennou, 2019). One nurse commented, “This tool in connection with the resident’s personalised life project allows us to pay special attention to each resident and thus to individualise, as much as possible, the treatment because too often in an institution, we tend to act in a systematic way” , said a nurse. Another nurse added, “Thanks to these two tools, “Secure-Photography” and conversational hypnosis, accompanying residents who initially refuse personal care has become much easier. Moreover, it has rekindled my passion for my job, which had become monotonous.”
In case of hospitalisation An additional benefit of the Secure-Photography tool is its portability during hospital admissions. It can be included in the patient's transfer documentation, allowing hospital staff to display it in the room and thus have tailored conversation topics for each elderly patient with communication difficulties. This approach may help reduce wandering behaviour, the need for physical restraints, and potentially decrease the excessive administration of anxiolytic medications. Some examples The Secure-Photography provides clues to help build metaphors and stories that the nurse can tell during bed baths or other care procedures (such as dressings, injections, etc.). For instance, a very aggressive lady who usually “casts spells” at the nurses had a pleasant experience when she was invited to imagine her cat joining her in bed. Throughout the process, the old lady cuddled her imaginary cat while the nurses talked about a “cat’s wash” (metaphor), saying, “as he passed his paw slightly wet to have comfortable fur… quietly… calmly” (sprinkling). The bed bath was completed peacefully for both the lady and the nurses.
Another resident spoke quite frequently in a confused manner when discussing aeroplanes. He was said to have worked in naval aviation, according to the few known elements of his life history. During the bed bath, the nurse helped him imagine taking off in his plane, without knowing his exact function within the navy. She told him that the plane is in the clouds and the rain is falling, but the cockpit is not completely waterproof and that it is normal that he can feel a little water (metaphor), he is completely safe in his plane approaching the runway. The control tower has just asked you to “turn right and then left in order to land» (the nurse washed his back and changed the diaper). This metaphor continued until the resident was seated in his chair, with the nurse saying, “And there is a slight air hole that you know how to handle” when the hoist was used. The resident smiled at the end of the care, whereas he was usually disengaged. Assessment To assess the effectiveness of this tool during challenging care situations, the team manager can establish specific targets in the nursing records. This allows analysing the specific behaviour of a patient over an extended period, as well as facilitating comparative analysis between units where this tool is employed and those where it is not.The physician can also monitor the reduction in sedative drug usage for individual residents, as well as compare medication utilisation across different units that have or have not integrated this tool. Conclusion: Employing hypnosis techniques with elderly people who have cognitive impairments related to neurodegenerative diseases provides comfort for both residents and nurses. By developing tailored metaphors for the elderly, nurses can mobilise their creativity and caring values.This work can also form part of constructing the resident's individual care plan, reconnecting them with their life history. Involving the family in the search for relevant photographs makes them active participants in the care home admission process, which can often be emotionally challenging. ￼23
Specificity of Trance in Elderly Individuals with Neurocognitive Disorders Hypnosis can be a valuable tool for managing behavioural disturbances and improving the quality of life in elderly individuals with neuro-cognitive disorders (NCDs). However, it is important to note that trance in these individuals’ presents certain peculiarities compared to trance in healthy adults. Healthcare professionals working with moderate to severe cases may find themselves challenged by the phenomenon of intermittent trances. Patients with attentional disorders may spontaneously exit the trance state, potentially returning to it quickly. It is crucial to become accustomed to this oscillation between wakefulness and trance. The key here is the persistence of hypnotic discourse. Due to these fluctuations, the trance level is often less profound, with less pronounced signs. While a trance state is typically characterized by decreased muscle tone, hyper-salivation, verbal deceleration, or spontaneous eye closure, in frail elderly individuals, one is more likely to observe a fixed gaze into space, reduced verbal production, or behavioural quiescence. As some individuals with NCDs may experience challenges in verbal communication, the hypnotherapist can utilize non- verbal communication techniques, such as touch and eye contact, to establish rapport with the individual and effectively convey instructions. Sessions tend to be shorter, though this does not necessarily indicate failure. The repetition of sessions throughout the day can be advantageous. These trances, which occur without external human intervention, are most often reactive to situations of psychological stress, such as pain, confusion, and anxiety. It is therefore common to find the individual already in a trance state prior to any intervention. Identifying these moments is crucial, as they provide an entry point for hypnotic work. The objective is to "enter the patient's reality" in order to guide the trance using therapeutic suggestions. Negative hypnotic loops can thus be transformed into positive ones.  References
Duff, S.C and Nightingale, D.J (2007). Alternative Approaches to Supporting Individuals With Dementia: Enhancing Quality of Life Through H y p n o s i s , A l z h e i m e r ' s C a r e To d a y 8 ( 4 ) : 3 2 1 - 3 3 1 , DOI:10.1097/01.ALCAT.0000297153.89903.7b
Wawrziczny E., Buquet, A., Picard S., Archives of Gerontolgy ans Geriatrics, Volume 96, September-october 2021, 104453 3.  Bertoni P., Repères en Gériatrie, mai 2020, vol. 22, numéro 185
   Sirvain S., Repères en Gériatrie, mars 2021, vol. 23, numéro 193 « beaucoup de patients âgés atteints de pathologies neurodégénératives sont dissociés, en transe quotidienne spontanée ou post-traumatique… Nombre de patients, bien que présents physiquement, semblent ailleurs dans leurs souvenirs, absents de notre réalité et de notre présent. Les regards sont tournés vers une intériorité. Cela ressemble bien à une présence/absence, à une dissociation. »
   Perennou G., L’hypnose pour accompagner les patients âgés, Bruxelles, Satas, 2017 6.  Perennou G., Métaphores hypnotiques pour accompagner les patients lors des soins techniques, Bruxelles, Satas, 2019
Perennou G., Sirvain S., Hypnose médicale de la personne âgée pour les professionnels de la santé. Dunod, 2024
Floccia M., Hypnoses en pratique gériatrique, Dunod, 2020
Floccia M., Cas pratiques en hypnose gériatrique, Dunod, 2024 ￼24
HYPNOSIS, AI AND GENERATIVE TECHNOLOGIES 25 The continuous innovations involving the world of AI are disrupting all fields of culture, work, entertainment, and science. Their improvement is accelerating fast, and every day, there are more things it can do better than us. There are risks, but the opportunities for human society are  enormous.
This means that the world of psychotherapy, clinics, and medicine - even more so,  hypnosis applied to these fields - needs continuous updating and deep reflection on the impact of  these tools  on both hypnotherapy clinicians and their patients.
This series of articles dedicated to the  revolution that AI is imposing on the world of science aims precisely to stimulate through examples, information, and metaphors a complex reflection on what impact this type of  technology is having and may have on the field of psychotherapy and hypnotic psychotherapy.
One of the most exciting questions may concern the impact of how we build and maintain rapport with our hypnosis patients in the future. In this regard, an old example can generate several extraordinarily fertile implications.
It concerns the very famous game 'GO.' Go is an ancient strategy board game originating in China. Its history dates back over 2,500 years,  making it one of the oldest games still played. According to legend, the Chinese emperor Yao  invented it to teach his son the principles of wisdom and discipline. Other accounts suggest that  generals used it to simulate battles and develop strategic skills.
Over the centuries, Go spread throughout East Asia, reaching Korea and Japan around the 7th  century. In Japan, the game became extremely popular, and a true cultural tradition developed  with the creation of specialized schools and the organization of tournaments. Japanese Go  peaked during the Edo period (1603-1868) when an official position of 'Meijin' (Grand Master of  Go) was established. In the 20th century, Go also began to gain popularity in the West, thanks to cultural exchanges  and the interest of game scholars.
Go organizations and federations were founded in various countries, and the game became an international activity with world tournaments. Well, artificial intelligence (AI) revolutionized the theories and practices of the ancient game of Go in profound ways, introducing new strategies and innovative approaches and transforming the landscape of this game. This revolution had its most significant symbolic moment in 2016, when AlphaGo, a program developed by DeepMind (an AI research company owned by Google),  defeated Lee Sedol, one of the best Go players in the world.
￼25 By Fabio Carnevale
This event demonstrated that AI could surpass human capabilities in a game considered among  the most complex due to its vastness of possible combinations and its need for strategic intuition. BUT above all, AI invented and applied ways of playing that no human had ever conceived in over  2000 years.
AlphaGo and subsequent AI programs, such as AlphaZero, developed and introduced  unexpected strategies that challenged the traditional principles of Go. AI led to the discovery of  new moves and sequences that initially seemed counterintuitive to human players but proved  highly effective. For example, according to classical game theory, AlphaGo preferred moves that seemed unconventional or weak but proved to be winning in the long run. These approaches  forced human players to reconsider their notions of opening and territorial control, leading to greater game experimentation and innovation.
There's more. Let me give to you another example: 'The Last Screenwriter" is the first film entirely written by artificial intelligence. Its premiere, scheduled for last June in London, was canceled due to the numerous controversies that were arising.
The film, directed by Peter Luisi, concerns the story of a screenwriter who realizes he is a less gifted screenwriter than artificial intelligence. A sort of meta-film.
To understand the significance of these themes, I propose three extraordinary examples of the application of AI in the field of human sciences and medicine. Among the most recent is Regina Barzilay from MIT ( Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence Laboratory Cambridge, USA) .
After her personal experience of breast cancer she created a significant  breakthrough in detecting early-stage breast cancer by AI. She also led the team that used  machine learning to discover Halicin, the first new antibiotic in 30 years.
David Silver is a Principal Research Scientist at Google DeepMind. He led the AlphaGo team that built the AI to defeat the world's best human Go player through sophisticated reinforcement learning processes. AIs use these processes to learn new concepts in a way that is completely  similar to how animals and humans do but with an infinitely faster learning speed.
The exciting thing is that, since you cannot give a computer a reinforcement similar to that used  for a dog or a dolphin (a cookie or a fish) or a human (a good grade or a compliment), to reinforce  the correct answers, machine learning occurs through a suitable algorithm or a lousy algorithm,  which have the value of OK or not OK.
Paolo Pirjanian founded Embodied and is a pioneer in developing emotionally intelligent robots to  aid child development. The goal of AIs capable of distinguishing human emotions has led  Pirjanian's team to prPirjanian's capable of training social skills in healthy human subjects and  subjects diagnosed within the autism spectrum. These robots train young humans in eye contact and turn-taking skills in complex social  relationships.
If you think these topics are science fiction or irrelevant, you just need to check how many publications there are constantly on PubMed regarding the use of AI in medicine, psychiatry, and psychotherapy to understand the significance of the revolution underway.
On the other hand, these tools continue to improve constantly through reinforcement learning  processes that occur 24 hours a day, seven days a week. This means their capabilities will have  already improved since you started reading this article. ￼26
The fact that these tools are evolving so quickly leads to a first dilemma: our ability to understand their significance and imagine their use is slower and requires more time for reflection to integrate them into our daily practices.
One of the most recent and interesting articles on this topic is "Quality and correctness of AI generated versus human-written abstracts in psychiatric research papers" published this year by Tien-Wei Hsu and colleagues in the journal " P s y c h i a t r y R e s e a r c h " h t t p s : / / pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39213714/ The article evaluated the ability of an artificial intelligence
(AI)-based chatbot to generate abstracts from academic psychiatric articles. Five experts evaluated the quality of the 40 abstracts (about half of which were written by the AI)  using a blinded approach.
The detected AI content was 31.48% for generated structured abstracts, 75.58% for unstructured abstracts, and 66.48% for active comparators' abstracts.
For quality, generated structured  abstracts were rated similarly to originals, but unstructured ones received significantly lower  scores. Experts rated 40% accuracy with structured abstracts, 73% with unstructured ones, and 77% for active comparators. However, 30% of AI-generated abstract conclusions were incorrect. ￼27
Report From CEPE 28 ￼28 Image by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C. By Andreas Kollar
Dear colleagues and members of the European Society of Hypnosis, I am deeply honored to assume the role of Chair for the Committee on Educational Programmes in Europe (CEPE). I am taking over from Randi Abrahamson, who has been instrumental in advancing our mission and will now focus her efforts on a core function within ESH's important research project evaluating the efficacy of hypnosis.. Our work at CEPE focuses on several key initiatives, including the accreditation of training societies (ATS) and the European Society of Hypnosis Certificate (ESHC). These programs are designed to ensure that hypnosis education maintains a high level of quality and integrity, offering practitioners the tools they need to excel in their field. Additionally, we aim to inspire societies to contribute to our shared training database, which serves as a resource to elevate educational standards across Europe. As I step into this position, I am committed to continuing the excellent work already underway. Together with my colleagues, we will work to expand the reach of our certification programs, simplify application processes, and promote innovative educational tools, such as our growing collection of videos and workshops. I am confident that, through these efforts, we will support both emerging and experienced professionals in their pursuit of excellence in hypnosis. I look forward to collaborating with all of you to further strengthen the educational foundation of hypnosis in Europe. ￼29
The World of Research 30 ￼30 Image by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C. Picture by F.C.
Research Reflections 31 Burkhard Peter (2024) has published a very interesting paper that he has titled Hypnosis in psychotherapy, psychosomatics and medicine. A brief overview. I’m not sure that the title does this article justice. It is effectively a history of hypnosis, but not one of those boring, ‘yet another one’ accounts! This one assesses the theories and practices in the context of the prevailing philosophies of the time and also evaluates them against more modern ideas. At 12 pages (not counting the copious references) it is not, as the title suggests, particularly brief, but it is a rewarding read. Rather than attempt a summary I will just follow through one of the issues it raised for me. Burkhard makes a distinction between the clinical use of hypnosis and its scientific investigation, although he does recommend more research studies (science) to evaluate the use of hypnosis in the medical field. The science/therapy distinction is very much tied to the issue of susceptibility. Therapists generally have neither the time nor, to be honest, the inclination to evaluate a patient’s hypnotisability. I have mentioned in these pages before that it is striking that so many clinicians have the impression that their clients don’t seem to include the non-susceptible. Where have the ‘lows’ gone? Is it that the scientists (who do see hypnotic lows) are actually working with a different process? This seems to me to be very unlikely. A more plausible explanation, I think, is that the anxiety involved (especially in a discipline such as dentistry) assists hypnosis by causing a shift in activity towards the right hemisphere (e.g. Abrahamsen & Naish, 2021). Burkhard himself comments upon the weak impact of hypnotic susceptibility upon therapeutic success rates, pointing out that many studies have used relatively few patients, an insufficient number to demonstrate strong statistical significance. This is true, but one could argue the reverse. In other words, if it takes a large number of people to demonstrate the facilitating effect of having higher hypnotic susceptibility, then perhaps this means that the advantage is very small – which is exactly what the studies show. There appear to be two possible explanations: either there is no need to be very hypnotisable to gain therapeutic benefit, or the presence of people of lower susceptibility, who, through hemispheric shifts have temporarily become rather higher, means that there is no longer a clear spread of susceptibility. This is in contrast to a scientist testing in a laboratory, where there is no hemispheric shift to blur the distinction. It would be nice if it were possible to have a simple and quick way of enhancing people’s susceptibility, and thereby produce a clearer, stronger link with favourable outcomes. With this in mind I was ￼31 By Peter Naish
interested to find a paper by David White and colleagues (White, Ciorciari, Carbis & Liley; 2009) an Australian group. They used virtual reality (VR) headsets with their participants, which delivered natural sounds and images (e.g. of rivers) while a spoken induction and suggestions were being given. In principle this sounds helpful, because it might assist those who struggle to generate rich imagery of their own. The authors believed that the VR was likely to boost the responsiveness of the lower scorers.
They do not mention the spread of hypnotisability across participants, but they do say that, rather than preselecting highs and lows, they merely split the participants into two, about the mid-range score, then compared the higher- and lower-scoring groups Unfortunately, most of the paper reports the impact of this hypnosis upon the EEG signals picked up by scalp electrodes, rather than exploring the specific contribution of the VR. There was a clear effect of hypnosis, showing that the VR technique worked, but not whether it was better than traditional hypnosis. We do know that VR alone can be used therapeutically. For example some success has been found in using immersive VR to treat soldiers suffering from PTSD. This form of VR presents a 360° world and allows movement. In that way a soldier can feel that he is advancing (in reality he is walking on a treadmill) into battle, with all the expected explosions etc.
However, hypnosis can be used for PTSD too, and it can generate all the same realistic effects (via hallucinations) as VR, but without the expensive equipment. What we have lacked is a study linking the two. There is a paper which reviews studies to date, in press in the IJCEH, by Mélanie Louras and a large team of collaborators. The paper does not confine itself to hypnosis, but explores the combination of VR with a range of ‘mind-body’ therapies, i.e. mindfulness, relaxation, meditation and hypnosis.
Curiously, hypnosis appears to be the only therapy that is not enhanced by the addition of VR. Perhaps this is because it is the only therapy that can generate its own virtual reality. Happy Hypnotising! References Abrahamsen, R. & Naish, P. (2021). Studies in patients with temporomandibular disorders pain: Can scales of hypnotic susceptibility predict the outcome on pain relief? American J. of Clinical H y p n o s i s , 6 4 ( 1 ) , 1 2 - 1 9 , D O I : 10.1080/00029157.2020.1863183
Louras, M. et al. (2024). Virtual reality combined with mind-body therapies for the management of pain: a scoping review. Intl. J. of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis, (in press). White, D., Ciorciari, J., Carbis, C. & Liley, D. (2009). EEG correlates of virtual reality hypnosis. Intl. J. of Clinical and Experimental Hypnosis, 57(1), 94-116, DOI: 10.1080/00207140802463690

THE USE OF ERICKSONIAN RAPID HYPNOSIS IN MEDICAL AND PSYCHOLOGICAL EMERGENCIES (PARTICULARLY IN TIMES OF NATURAL DISASTERS AND WAR)S 34 Recent natural disasters and escalating threats of wars and terrorist acts, have highlighted the critical need for civilian healthcare providers to receive proper training in pain and emergencies management principles. Ericksonian rapid hypnosis is crucial for medical doctors and psychologists in emergencies, especially during wartime, as it enables rapid psychological stabilization and pain management, facilitating immediate care under extreme stress. Ericksonian rapid hypnosis is characterized by its flexible, indirect, and patient-centred techniques, which are tailored to the individual's unique experiences and subconscious mind. Unlike traditional hypnosis, which often involves direct suggestions, Ericksonian hypnosis utilizes short metaphors and subtle suggestions to guide the patient into a trance state. When dealing with a patient during emergencies, the approach can be fast and particularly gentle, focused on using the patient's own resources to regain calm.
In the context of medical and psychological emergencies, particularly during wartime, Ericksonian rapid hypnosis is employed to quickly induce a state of focused attention and deep hypnosis. This modified state of consciousness allows for the rapid alleviation of acute symptoms such as traumatic injuries, pain, anxiety, panic attacks, and traumas. The approach is particularly valuable in high-stress situations and emergencies, where conventional psychological and medical interventions such as anaesthesia, may be limited or delayed. Through the use of Ericksonian communication and techniques, doctors and psychologists can help individuals tap into the patients inner resources, fostering resilience and promoting medical and psychological symptom relief during disasters. The adaptability of this method makes it especially effective in unpredictable and chaotic environments, offering a powerful tool for both immediate relief and longer-term recovery in times of crisis. Here are some examples of medical and psychological emergencies that can arise during wartime and can benefit from rapid medical hypnosis. Medical emergencies can benefit from rapid medical hypnosis particularly in acute pain, severe traumas and emergency medical procedures when supportive pharmacological approach and anaesthetic procedures are not available.
THE MEDICAL EMERGENCIES Traumatic Injuries: When the patient presents with acute trauma and is conscious, it is very important that they do not become agitated and do not have panic attacks, so as not to worsen the clinical situation. Moreover, in these cases, rapid hypnosis can provide important pain analgesia, and if necessary, we can induce local anaesthesia in the patient. Blast Injuries: Resulting from explosions, causing multiple traumas including amputations, burns, and internal injuries. Gunshot Wounds: Penetrating trauma that can lead to severe bleeding, organ damage, and infection. Shrapnel Injuries: Lacerations and deep tissue damage from fragmented objects. Crush Injuries: Occurring when buildings collapse or heavy machinery falls on individuals, leading to fractures, compartment syndrome, and potential rhabdomyolysis. Bone fractures: it is very important that the patient with bone fractures does not become agitated and does not have panic attacks, so as not to worsen the clinical situation. Head traumas: in emergencies involve potentially life-threatening injuries that can lead to brain damage, bleeding, or swelling, requiring immediate medical attention. If the head injury patient does not lose consciousness, it is important that they do not become agitated and do not have panic attacks in order not to make the situation worse. ￼34 By Paola Brugnoli
Burns: Thermal Burns: From explosions, fires, or chemical exposure, leading to severe skin and tissue damage. Chemical Burns: Caused by exposure to chemical weapons or industrial chemicals, leading to tissue destruction and systemic toxicity. Radiation Sickness: Acute Radiation Syndrome: Following nuclear attacks or accidents, symptoms range from nausea and vomiting to bone marrow failure and death, depending on the radiation dose. THE PSYCHOLOGICAL EMERGENCIES Acute Stress Reaction: Combat Stress Reaction: Immediate psychological and physiological response to the stress of combat, including anxiety, confusion, and hypervigilance, potentially leading to impaired decision- making and combat effectiveness. Traumatic and Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD): Flashbacks and Nightmares: Recurring, intrusive memories of traumatic events, leading to severe anxiety, depression, and risk of suicide. Hyperarousal: Constant state of alertness, difficulty sleeping, and exaggerated startle responses, impairing daily functioning. Moral Injury and stress: Guilt and Shame: Resulting from actions taken or witnessed during combat that violate personal or societal moral codes, leading to deep psychological distress, depression, and suicidal ideation. Severe Depression: Suicidal Ideation: Persistent thoughts of suicide, hopelessness, and withdrawal from others, exacerbated by the horrors of war and loss of loved ones. Panic Attacks: A panic attack is a sudden, intense surge of fear or anxiety accompanied by physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, shortness of breath, and dizziness. Debilitating Anxiety: Sudden onset of intense fear, heart palpitations, and shortness of breath, triggered by the stress of combat or reminders of traumatic events. Substance Abuse: Alcoholism and Drug Addiction: Used as a coping mechanism for the stress and trauma of war, leading to dependency, health complications, and impaired judgment in critical situations. Alcoholism and drug addiction can be considered medical and psychological emergencies due to the severe and potentially life-threatening consequences they can have on an individual's physical and mental health. Here’s why: Overdose Risk: Excessive consumption of alcohol or drugs can lead to an overdose, which can be fatal. An overdose can cause respiratory failure, heart attack, seizures, or loss of consciousness, all of which require immediate medical intervention.
Withdrawal Symptoms: When someone who is dependent on alcohol or drugs suddenly stops using them, they can experience withdrawal symptoms. In severe cases, withdrawal can lead to life-threatening conditions like delirium tremens (DTs) in alcoholics, characterized by seizures, hallucinations, and severe confusion. Suicidal Ideation: Individuals struggling with addiction often experience severe depression, anxiety, and hopelessness. These feelings can lead to suicidal thoughts or attempts, requiring immediate psychological intervention to prevent self-harm or suicide. Psychosis: Certain drugs can induce psychosis, characterized by hallucinations, delusions, and disordered thinking. This can lead to dangerous behaviours that put the individual and others at risk, necessitating emergency psychiatric care. Aggression and Violence: Addiction can sometimes trigger aggressive or violent behaviour, especially when the person is under the influence or in withdrawal. This can lead to situations that require urgent intervention to protect the individual and others. Impaired Decision-Making: Chronic substance abuse can impair cognitive functions, leading to poor decision-making and risky behaviours. This might include unsafe sexual practices, sharing needles, or engaging in criminal activities, all of which can have immediate and severe consequences. Psychosis: Psychoses in cases of severe stress can quickly arise even in non-psychiatric patients. It is therefore very helpful to help these patients with an emergency hypnotic approach Hallucinations and Delusions: Severe disconnection from reality, potentially caused by extreme stress, lack of sleep, or head trauma, leading to unpredictable and dangerous behaviour. All these emergencies require immediate and specialized care, often in challenging and resource-limited environments. The psychological impacts can be as debilitating as physical injuries and may have long-lasting effects on individuals and communities, if it is not taken care of from the outset. Here are the fundamental points of rapid hypnosis approach during medical and psychological emergencies: Rapport Building: Establish a strong sense of connection and trust with the patient. This creates a safe environment where the patient feels understood and supported. Utilization: Use the patient’s current experience and environment. For example, if they are breathing heavily, the therapist might say, "As you notice your breath, you might ￼35
find a rhythm that feels more calming." This helps the patient use their own sensations and perceptions to move towards relaxation. Pacing and Leading: Start by matching the patient's current state (pacing), then gradually guide them to a calmer state (leading). For instance, if the patient is speaking rapidly, the therapist might initially speak at a similar pace before gradually slowing down. Indirect Suggestions: Offer calming suggestions indirectly, allowing the patient’s subconscious mind to process them without resistance. For example, instead of saying, "You should calm down," the therapist might say, "Some people find that as they listen to the sound of their breath, they begin to feel a bit more at ease." Embedded Commands: Use embedded commands within normal conversation to promote relaxation. For instance, "You might find that as you continue to sit here, you can begin to feel more comfortable." Metaphors and Stories: Share calming metaphors or stories that resonate with the patient’s experience. For example, telling a story about a storm that eventually passes can help the patient visualize their panic attack as something temporary. Focus Shifting: Gently redirect the patient’s attention from their panic symptoms to something neutral or positive. For example, encourage them to notice the texture of their clothing or the sound of the wind. Double Binds: Offer choices that both lead to the desired outcome. For example, "Would you like to sit here or there while you start to feel more at ease?" This gives the patient a sense of control while still guiding them towards relaxation. Positive Reframing: Help the patient view their symptoms in a less threatening light. For instance, "Your body is giving you signals that it’s time to take a moment to rest and recover." Amnesia and Time Distortion (time-line): Suggest that the intense feelings will soon fade or be forgotten. For instance, "In a short while, you might barely remember how intense this feeling was." Example of Instructions for Rapid Medical Hypnosis Induction for Anaesthesia and Acute Pain Relief in emergency Ensure the patient is in a comfortable position (sitting or lying down). Use a calm, soothing tone throughout the induction. Adjust the pace based on the patient's responses. Aim for a rhythm and voice modulation that promotes relaxation, calm and peace. Therapist: "Alright, I want you to focus on your breathing for a moment. Take a deep breath in... and let it out slowly... That's right... With each breath in, imagine you're breathing in calm, soothing energy... and with each breath out, let go of any tension or discomfort... Just let it melt away... Now, I want you to focus all your attention on a spot right in front of you... It could be on the ceiling or wall... Just pick one spot and stare at it... As you keep your eyes on that spot, you'll notice your eyelids becoming heavier and heavier... With every breath you take, your eyes become more and more relaxed... In a moment, I'm going to count down from 5 to 1... With each number, you'll feel yourself becoming more deeply relaxed... more comfortable... more at ease... and as I count down from 5 to 1, you will begin to feel a pleasant warmth (or pleasant cold) instead of pain... until the place of your pain will gradually become more and more numb and warm... more and more numb and warmer... warmer and warmer.... And when I reach 1, your eyes will close, and you'll be in a state of deep relaxation… deep relaxation… deep relaxation… where your mind and body can focus on warmth and healing... 5... Feel your body starting to relax... Letting go of any tension in your muscles... and letting go for any pain…. Letting go… letting go… and you feel more and more warm… more and more warm… more and more warm… more and more numb and warmer... warmer and warmer.... 4... Your eyelids are getting heavier now... They want to close... It's okay to let them close... 3... Feeling more relaxed, calm, and peaceful... Deeper and deeper... the pain becomes more and more a sensation of increasingly pleasant warmth, instead of localised pain... warmer and warmer… Letting go of any tension in your muscles... and letting go for any pain…. Letting go… letting go… and you feel more and more warm… more and more warm… more and more warm… more and more numb and warmer... warmer and warmer.... 2... Almost there... Just let go completely... Your eyes are so heavy... heavy and relaxed… heavy and relaxed… and nothing else matters any more... only your relaxed body... ever more pleasantly relaxed... ever more pleasantly falling... warm and numb... pain-free... only warm… warm… 1... Close your eyes now... and drift into deep, deep relaxation... Deeper and deeper... the pain becomes more and more a sensation of increasingly pleasant warm… pleasant warm… pleasant warm…. instead of localised pain... warmer and warmer… warmer and warmer… warmer and warmer… Now that you're feeling calm and relaxed, I want you to imagine a warm, soothing light... It's a healing light, and it begins to glow at the top of your head... This light represents comfort, peace, and relief... It's moving down slowly... spreading through your body... relaxing every muscle, every ￼36
As the light reaches the area where you feel pain, imagine it surrounding that area... bathing it in a warm, soothing glow... The pain begins to melt away... dissolve... just like ice in warm sunlight... With each breath, the light grows stronger... the relief becomes more profound... You might even feel a pleasant numbness in that area now... as the discomfort fades away... Every breath you take reinforces this feeling of comfort and relief... In this state, your body is healing... repairing... and restoring balance... You're safe... you're calm... and you're in control... and the medical procedures we do now on your body give you a sense of warmth... warmth... more and more warmth and relaxation.... warmth... warmth... more and more warmth and relaxation.... Stay in this comfortable state for as long as you need... When you're ready to return, I'll guide you back, feeling refreshed and with a sense of relief and ease..." In emergencies, the author chooses to offer the patient a ‘pleasantly warm’ feeling and metaphor, because during a trauma, whether due to bleeding or stress, the patient feels very cold, which generally increases muscle contracture, pain and stress. The duration of this hypnotic state can be adjusted based on the patient’s needs. When the session is over, gradually bring the patient out of hypnosis by counting up from 1 to 5, suggesting they will feel relaxed, refreshed, and pain-free, as if the pleasant warmth they can  continue to feel will induce local anaesthesia. We can also teach the patient, with post-hypnotic suggestions, to achieve these sensations by themselves. Note: The author Dr. Maria Paola Brugnoli, MD, PhD, worked for five years as a medical doctor on Italian Red Cross Emergency Medical Service on ambulances, and twenty years as a medical doctor and researcher at the Department of Anaesthesia, Intensive Care and Pain Therapy at the University of Verona, Verona, Italy. She has often used these techniques in the emergency context. References:

BSMDH Training
By Kathleen Long The British Medical and Dental Association held the first part of its annual foundation course on 17th/18th August 2024 in the House for an Art Lover in Glasgow. Most of the participants were dental and we see a big increase in the interest in hypnosis from our dental colleagues. Although the course is held in Glasgow we have had participants from several other countries like Canada, Germany and the Netherlands Some come to combine an ancestry hunt or a holiday with hypnosis training. During the course we cover basic techniques, objectives, history, ethics and misconceptions about hypnosis and there is plenty of time for practice, questions and interaction, metaphor and storytelling as well as the short techniques that can be very useful in children are also covered, there are also hours built into the course of self-study and homework assignments. Our second module was  held on 21st/22nd September solidifying what the participants had previously learned and helped build  confidence and enthusiasm for them to put their  new skills into practice. Our lecturers are also  part of a mentoring program. The glove hand anaesthesia demonstration proved popular. One of the participants was brave enough to volunteer for the demonstration I did with a  substantial sized needle. Two  great weekends and  looking forward to hearing about their success with their new hypnosis skills.
IPMOMED (ITALY) - BY Andrea Di Ruvo - Head of Psychologist Training at IPNOMED Andrea Di Ruvo is a psychologist and psychotherapist with a deep passion for hypnosis. Initially, he became interested in hypnosis in a clinical setting, using it to manage pain and anxiety for patients undergoing diagnostic and therapeutic procedures. Later, he realised that hypnosis could also be a valuable tool in psychotherapy, which led him to pursue a diploma at the European School of Hypnotic Psychotherapy in Milan, where he wrote a thesis on psychosomatics. Andrea is particularly fascinated by the connection between mind and body, and how the unconscious mind, as his mentor M.H. Erickson referred to it, can influence our lives. His main areas of expertise include hypnotic psychotherapy, psychosomatics, and clinical hypnosis, with a special focus on using hypnosis to support women during childbirth. He has participated in international conferences on the use and application of hypnosis and has authored several articles on the topic. Since 2023, Andrea has been a lecturer for Ipnomed.
￼
When the Educational Director of IPNOMED asked me to participate in the XXII World Congress of Clinical and Medical Hypnosis, I felt deeply honoured, yet simultaneously apprehensive. I was to bring Pino Regaldo’s voice to the world’s scholars on two significant topics: 1) The hypnotic hallucination influences the hypothalamic regulation of appetite
2) Effects of self-hypnosis in promoting weight loss in obese patients Crossing the threshold of the Congress felt like entering a temple of knowledge. I felt a profound sense of responsibility in carrying forward the legacy of Giuseppe (Pino) Regaldo, a pioneer of hypnosis in Italy, who was deeply committed to these two studies but whose illness prevented him from living long enough to present them himself. The studies primarily focused on the hormonal variations that hypnosis can induce in treated subjects. In essence, they delved into the power of the mind over the body— the central theme for which Pino Regaldo dedicated his life and passed on through his teachings. Could I avoid this responsibility? Absolutely not!
And so, with honour and a touch of fear, I immersed myself in studying the articles, research, and materials to prepare my presentation in English for the Congress. Even during the preparation, I could sense the importance of what I was about to do. The interest and curiosity that drove me to further explore the topic of hypnosis were aligned with the very spirit of Pino Regaldo’s teachings, pushing me to become ever more skilled. And… Ready, set, go! Once I arrived in Krakow, Poland, the Congress’s venue, I attended the gala dinner and various workshops. I engaged with people from all over Europe, listening to numerous testimonies, studies, ￼40
and valuable materials to help me grow both professionally and personally. On the day of my presentation, I saw the room filled with people as I approached the podium for a final review of my prepared materials. Someone asked me,
“Where is your colleague?”
“Wait, he is part of the story!” The theme of international cooperation provided the perfect backdrop for presenting the results of our studies on the impact of hypnosis in treating obesity. It was a team effort, the result of months of research and passion, demonstrating how hypnosis also operates on a hormonal level. The room was packed with colleagues from around the world, each bringing a unique wealth of experiences and knowledge. My presentation was met with great interest, but what struck me most were the audience’s questions.
Their curiosity was not directed where I had expected. I believed that the influence of the mind aided by hypnosis on the body would be the central focus of the studies I was presenting.
Instead, the audience’s attention centred on the compliance that participants had shown in adhering to the study.
This led me to reflect on how it was the personalities of Regaldo and the involved collaborators that attracted and catalysed such adherence. It further made me ponder the fundamental role of the therapeutic relationship. Hypnosis works, yes! But it works even better if I, as the practitioner, truly believe in it! I think this is the most important lesson Regaldo left us, in line with what Milton Erickson used to say: believe in it! I believe in it, and I am ready to apply all my professionalism to pass on everything I have learned, so that others may believe in it as much as I do and experience the same satisfaction I have as a psychologist and psychotherapist. This way, our patients can continue to improve. Regaldo taught us that trust is the cornerstone upon which every effective therapy is built. His ability to forge a deep and authentic connection with his patients was admirable. I am convinced that this is precisely the key to unlocking the potential of hypnosis. Returning from the event, I brought back not only new knowledge but also a renewed awareness of the value of our profession.
Hypnosis is a powerful tool, capable of transforming lives. And we, as therapists, have the privilege of guiding our patients through this journey of growth and change. So, thank you for this opportunity, for this Congress, and for teaching me once again that IPNOMED is doing tremendous work for people, for all healthcare professionals, and for all our patients. ￼41
International Festival of Storytelling Vallendar Germany 6.9.2024-8.9.2024 By Kathleen Long It was lovely to be invited to attend and deliver workshops at the International Festival of Therapeutic Storytelling for a second time. It was wonderfully run as usual by Stefan Hammel and jointly supported by the Institute of Hypno-Systemic Consultation and IMEL Luxembourg. Unfortunately Marie Jeanne Bremner from IMHEL was unable to be there this year due to a prior commitment.
My German is very poor so many of the workshops were beyond my limited linguistic skills. There was a very interesting one on Tinnitus, which I suffer from, and luckily, I was able to catch up with the presenter Peter Schneider during dinner to find out more about his research into the subject. I presented a workshop on the power of the mirror and the importance of processing cues in trance work.
Laurence Sugarman presented a workshop on Cultivating the Trance of the Encounter with techniques to cultivate and stimulate  plasticity and inner searches for those in our care. Laurence is not only a well-known and respected hypnotherapist and researcher but a fantastic musician as well and his skills were put to good use during  the social time in the evening.
Christine Guilloux gave a fascinating workshop titled Eyes Wide Shut, Eyes Wide Open showing new ways to approach a problem we wanted to solve. Christine has recently lost her partner of more than 41 years ; Bernard Devin and it says a lot about her devotion to her craft that she still came to participate and share her many years of expertise at the festival.
The accommodation was very peaceful with lovely gardens and in the evenings, everyone participated in the impromptu events. There was story telling , Lawence Sugarman and other artistes making great music together as well as some singing and magic. Without Marie Jeanne’s presence I felt unable to sing Frere Jacques and delivered a rendition of an old Irish favourite called Danny Boy.
Stefan , who is a master of storytelling, once again organised and delivered an event that was educational , intimate and bonding.  I loved every minute of it.
The next festivals of therapeutic storytelling in Vallendar (near Koblenz), Germany are on Oct 10-12, 2025 and Sept 4-6, 2026. Information via stefan.hammel @ hsb-westpfalz.de or www.erzaehl- festival.de. ￼
￼
￼42
65th jubilee of the Society for Hypnosis in Finland TH-VH
Ritva Ruotsalainen & Aleksi Jalava Society for Hypnosis in Finland TH-VH was founded 65 years ago, and in January 2024 this was celebrated at a three-day (Friday - Sunday) jubilee seminar in Ruka village ski resort at the foot of Rukatunturi mountain, in Northern Finland. The seminar location was chosen in response to some members' wish that the association's events would sometimes be held elsewhere than in southern Finland and the capital. The participants came from different parts of Finland: there were thirty participants present and the same number of remote participants. Two former presidents of the association were present (Lea Polso and Martti Tenkku) in addition to the current president Aleksi Jalava. The program was diverse and the landscape around was magically rugged.  Although it was beautiful outside, the extreme cold prevented the first part of the program from being held outdoors. The board member Nina Mömmö guided the shamanic “drum expedition” with a shaman's drum she had made herself. The seminar also ended on Sunday with a drum show, when we got to experience a shaman performance by shaman Satu Palosaari from Kuusamo village. Nina told that when she had started looking for a shaman for our jubilee seminar, she got so interested in shamanism herself that she had participated in shamanic courses and also made her own drum. I myself didn't get to participate in Nina's drum performance, but on Sunday I got to experience Satu's magical shamanic “drum expedition”.  At first, the shaman sang a poem of her own, after which we could fall into our own imagery in the arms of a peaceful drum beat. Many images crossed my mind, the passage of time calmed down. At the end, it was possible to ask questions. The experience was dreamy and gentle. Sunday's seminar program also included academic shaman researcher Helena Karhu's interesting and thought-provoking lecture on Nordic shamanism, typical for the region where we were. The lecture discussed, for example, the animism of shamanism worldview, holy places and spells. Helena also told about her shamanic courses. After this Finnish Dr. Sakari Kallio (currently at Skövde university, Sweden) lectured about altered states of consciousness. The similarities between hypnosis and the trance associated with shamanism are indeed thought provoking. Friday's pre seminar program also included lectures from Helsinki university's 3,5 years "Cognitive Hypnotherapy Psychotherapy Training Course" of the students' 2023 degree works. Freshly graduated cognitive hypnotherapists Ea Huhtala, Nina Kontula and Elina Gambardella talked about their learning experiences and results and findings of their degree works. We learned about successful cases of hypnosis and cognitive hypnotherapy in the treatment of fear of childbirth, in the treatment of insomnia and as part of anorexia nervosa treatment. The ones who presented their work felt that the use of hypnosis in addition to their own work was useful and they intend to continue using hypnotherapeutic techniques, and partly also commit to research. Saturday started with a very interesting program. The association's board had found a previously unpublished dictation based manuscript on hypnosis and its use. It had been dictated by our association's founding member Dr. Claës Cedercreutz in the early 1980's. The manuscript only resurfaced recently and the board is now working on its future publication. Association's secretary Pia Puolakka presented the manuscript's table of contents and told how the work on its editing had progressed. ￼43
Cedercreutz, who had extensive experience about the therapeutic use of hypnosis, had recorded detailed information about the treatment of different disorders with hypnosis. He was a famous surgeon in his time, but he also used a lot of hypnosis - and founded a youth circus in Hamina town and in the beginning directed it. We also had the opportunity to hear memoirs of Cedercreutz's daughter and son, both interviewed by our current and past presidents. They told  stories about their father's phases, about starting hypnosis, medical work and later life. The public was intensely interested in the book and the whole subject. Such a cultural act wonderfully celebrates the jubilee year of the great association! On Saturday afternoon, there was free time of a few hours, when one could enjoy (the very cold) outdoor life as much as one wanted, or stay in the seminar room watching the 1930's hypnosis themed movie Svengali. We learned that this movie kind of belongs for the general education of every person practicing hypnosis! Some went cross country skiing, some visited a reindeer farm, admiring the blue-toned mountain landscape and snow-covered spruce trees. Some went downhill skiing – after all we were just a few steps away from the slope and the ski lift. The atmosphere of  Ruka village and ski resort was international, people had come to the area from many different countries all over the world. After the afternoon free time break we returned to the seminar hall, as it was time for a nearly three-hour long keynote lecture. Dr. Eric Spiegel's energetic and perceptive lecture ”Hypnotic strategies for evoking attachment and relationship in the treatment of trauma: An introduction”. As the name suggests, the lecture dealt with various topics regarding attachment patterns, their causes, consequences and treatment. Eric uses some puns and a lot of repetition in his group hypnosis. On the other hand, he said that an exact understanding of the words is not necessary, there is also a lot of nonverbal communication. The comparison to old spells came to my mind...  On Saturday evening, the association offered the participants a hearty meal with Lapland style delicacies and wine. Evening get-together and dinner in the seminar hall took untill 10 p.m., just like on Friday, and after that some of us moved on to continue the evening in various bars and clubs of Ruka ski resort. In addition to having interesting conversations, the members went enriching the culture of the region – at least by dancing and singing. On the morning of the last day, before the shamanic lectures, we heard a presentation of the current writing of the history of the association. Physiatrist Timo Pehkonen of the board talked about some of the interviews of the senior, distinguished members, for example past presidents Matti-Pekka Harri and Hannu Lauerma among many others. After the actual seminar program there was a screening of documentary depicting the (in)famous, very well known in his time, Finnish lay hypnotist of 1970's-1980's ” Olliver Hawk” as he called himself by his artist name (indeed his real name was Erkki Olavi Hakasalo). The documentary was special and thought-provoking in so many ways. The association offered afternoon coffee and snacks during the movie.
The jubilee seminar weekend was lively and well- planned and organized coverage of great topics and of interesting performances. The participants got a wide perspective on hypnosis, shamanism & altered states of consciousness and got to learn about people who have influenced the field of hypnosis in Finland. The surroundings were enchanting and it was nice to be for once in a different sort of an educational environment as usually. We met old and new acquaintances. The participants also got the chance to enjoy many alternative self-directed leisure activities during the days. A lot had been made to fit in one weekend. And the association is going to organize even more interesting activities still later this year. Thank you! ￼44
French Corner 45 ESHNL French Corner, Gérard Fitoussi, 2022 08, English version European Congress in Antalya in October 2023, the CFHTB Forum in May 2024, and the ISH Congress in Krakow. Journals Hypnosis and Brief Therapies, Issue 73, Métawalk Editions, May-June-July 2024  This summer issue, titled "Questioning Our Practices," invites us to reflect on our use of hypnosis: what we do when we practice hypnosis, how we can improve, and the mistakes to avoid. It offers a moment of introspection about our practice, making it a thought-provoking read. The Journal of Hypnosis and Health, Issue 27, Dunod, April 2024  Philosophy takes center stage in this issue, featuring two articles: one by Matteo Bussoletti, referencing Arthur Schopenhauer (1788-1860) and exploring how his ideas can benefit healthcare professionals; the other is a dialogue between Oleg Poliakow and Christian Martens on the phenomenological approach to care.
The main focus of this issue is on workplace suffering and the evolving role of hypnosis in this area. ￼45 By Gérard Fitoussi
VERSION FRANÇAISE
Nous entrons dans une période de pause dans l’organisation des grands congrès après le congrès européen à Antalya en octobre,2023, le forum de la CFHTB en mai 2024 et le congrès de l’ISH à Cracovie.
Les revues Hypnose et thérapies brèves, numéro 73, éditions Métawalk, mai-juin-juillet 2024. Interroger nos pratiques, tel est le titre de ce numéro d’été. Que faisons-nous lorsque nous pratiquons l’hypnose, comment l’hypnose, comment pouvons-nous mieux faire, quelles sont les erreurs a éviter. Ce numéro d’été nous propose un retour sur nous-même et notre pratique, à lire et à méditer. La Revue de l’hypnose et de la santé, numéro 27, Dunod, avril 2024 La philosophie est à l’honneur dans ce numéro, avec deux articles l’un de Matteo Bussoletti en référence à Arthur Schopenhauer (1788-1860) et à ce que sa réflexion peut apporter au soignant et l’autre sur un plan différent est un dialogue entre Oleg Poliakow et Christan Martens sur l’approche phénoménologique du soin. Le dossier de ce numéro est consacré à la souffrance au travail et à l’utilisation de l’hypnose dans ce domaine en pleine évolution. ￼46
HYPNOSIS WITHOUT BORDERS NEWS A zoom meeting of the board members elected in Krakow was held on 19th August 2024 at 8pm CET. We agreed to investigate the setting up of a charity and to make some minor changes to the constitution.
It is important if this initiative is to succeed that we have good contact with both ESH and ISH members.
If you are interested in receiving information about HWB can you please let Central Office have your contact details so that we can keep you up to date on any progress we make.
Borys Ivnyev the president of the Ukrainian society tabled a paper which will be useful to help inform future help that HWB offers to those populations in crisis.
There is no doubt that HWB will need to be able to raise funds in future to help provide high quality resources to populations in crisis.
A big thank you to all of you who have given your time and expertise freely to help make this project a reality. By Kathleen Long ￼47 47
Calendar of Events 48 EVENTS PREDICTIVE CODING, AI, AND B E Y O N D : W H AT C O M P U TAT I O N A L NEUROSCIENCE CAN TELL US ABOUT CHANGING OUR MINDS Join us for this educative event to gain an insight into the fascinating life of Professor Karl Friston, a theoretical neuroscientist and authority on brain imaging. He invented statistical parametric mapping (SPM), voxel-based morphometry (VBM) and dynamic causal modeling (DCM). These contributions were motivated by schizophrenia research and theoretical studies of value-learning – formulated as the disconnection hypothesis of schizophrenia. Mathematical contributions include variational Laplacian procedures and generalised filtering for hierarchical Bayesian model inversion. Friston currently works on models of functional integration in the human brain and the principles that underlie neuronal interactions. https://www.rsm.ac.uk/events/hypnosis-and- psychosomatic-medicine/2023-24/hys02/ When 23/09/2024, 6:00 pm - 8:00 pm Details Join us for this educative event to gain an insight into the fascinating life of Professor Karl Friston, a theoretical neuroscientist and authority on brain imaging. He invented statistical parametric mapping (SPM), voxel-based morphometry (VBM) and dynamic causal modeling (DCM). These contributions were motivated by schizophrenia research and theoretical studies of value-learning – formulated as the disconnection hypothesis of schizophrenia. Mathematical contributions include variational Laplacian procedures and generalised filtering for hierarchical Bayesian model inversion. Friston currently works on models of functional integration in the human brain and the principles that underlie neuronal interactions. https://www.rsm.ac.uk/events/hypnosis-and- psychosomatic-medicine/2023-24/hys02/ E V E N T S U N V E I L I N G T H E M I N D : C O N V E R G E N C E O F H Y P N O T I C A N D PSYCHEDELIC REALITIES A TWO-DAY SCIENTIFIC CROSSTALK BETWEEN RESEARCHERS AND THERAPISTS 04/10/2024 - 05/10/2024  In the evolving landscape of psychotherapy, the integration of psychedelics and hypnotic  techniques—centred on the principles of suggestibility, absorption, and hypnotisability—  presents novel opportunities for advancing therapeutic methodologies. The Netherlands  Society for Hypnosis (NVvH) invites you to a symposium that seeks to bridge the gap between  these two approaches that aim to explore the depths of human consciousness. Key Themes:  • Intersecting Realms: Discover the parallels and distinctions between the altered states  induced by psychedelics, such as MDMA, psilocybin, DMT, LSD, and ketamine, and those  achieved through hypnosis. Explore how these states offer different paths to healing and  self-discovery.  • Unique Brain Mechanisms: Learn about the latest research showing how each practice  activates distinct brain mechanisms, offering unique insights into the nature of human  consciousness and therapeutic potential.  • Critical Factors in Therapy: Explore how suggestibility, absorption, dissociation, and the  ￼48
exploration of the unconscious shape therapeutic outcomes, and how these factors can be  used in both hypnosis and psychedelic therapy. We would like to invite you to join the new platform for networking in the hypnosis community world-wide: www.whoISHwho.com.
Please send us your CV, photo and if you have also your publication list to us at contact@whoishwho.com. This platform will also be used for organizing ISH congresses more easily. In the future, for example, if the planning committee needs to see the CV to learn about the experience of a potential speaker, his or her CV would already be easily available on the whoISHwho platform. Also if you are moderator planning a symposium and want to identify potential co-presenters, you could use the whoISHwho platform to identify theseindividuals. Of course, there are many more possibilities for encouraingnetworking in our community (and getting information about you to otherlike-minded people) for such a system. You also can upload your profile yourself, which makes it easier for us. But please - do not hesitate to just send us things by email and we will do this for you. ISH President ￼li
